  5.4 Technical Appendix

 5.4.1 Loss Control Procedure

      Loss of fluid returns will lower the hydrostatic head of the drilling fluid in the wellbore, thereby inducing a kick. The
       influx fluid will then flow to the surface or into the zone of lower pressure. Lost circulation can occur in naturally
       occurring fractured, vuggy, cavernous, sub-normally pressured or pressure depleted formations. Induced losses can
      occur from mechanical fracturing due to pressure surges while breaking circulation. In all cases of lost circulation,
       attempts should be made to keep the hole full. The hole can be filled with either light drilling fluid or water. A record
       of the amount of fluid pumped should be made.
     The concentration of LCM will vary according to losses. In the event of seepage losses, treat system with a finer grade
       of LCM at 20 – 30 Kg/m³. In the event of moderate to high rate of well bore loss, prepare pills at different graded
       concentrations and spot over thief zone. The concentration may vary between 18 to 70 lb/bbl. In order to avoid LCM
      plugging MWD tools a circulation sub should be installed in the drill string. The MWD engineer is to advise on the
       size and concentration of LCM that can be pumped through MWD Tools. Ensure that the drilling displace the LCM pill
       out of the string prior to shutting down the mud pumps.
      Important Note: Please note below concentration and recommended materials may be change with same
      materials on rig site.

       In case of having circulation losses:

       Losses less than 10 bbls/hr:
                -   Active system to be loaded with 10 lb/bbl of Fiber Fine.
       Losses 10- 40 bbls/hr:
                -   Active mud to be loaded with 15- 20 lb/bbl of Fiber Medium.
                -   Sweep the hole frequently with 50 bbls high viscous pill contains;
                -   15 lb/bbl Mica Medium - Corse.
                -   10 lb/bbl Walnut Shells Medium -Fine.
                -   10 lb/bbl Oyster Shell Fine.
       Losses 40 – 100 bbl/hr:
                 -  Stop Drilling.
                 -  Pull out string above the loss zone
                 -  Spot 100 bbls pill contains;
                 -  30 lb/bbl Fiber coarse.
                 -  30 lb/bbl Walnut Fine.
                 -  20 lb/bbl Mica Med.
                 -  Repeat the previous pill minimum of two times.

        Losses more than 100 bbls:

                -  Up to company procedure, may be squeeze 50 bbls of Cement squeeze.

Mixing LCM Pills

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
           South Azadegan Oilfield Project

   Contract No:          F – 20 Well Drilling Program                  Page 92 of 181

      The amount of LCM that can be added to a mud depends very much on the mud properties. High gels and YP
       will help keep the material in suspension. Since LCM is generally of low gradient, excess material tends to float
      on top if too much is added. The following procedure may prove useful for mixing LCM pills and avoiding the
      problem of flotation.

        1. ENSURE the pill has enough viscosity to keep the low gradient material in suspension before addition of
     LCM. ADD appropriate viscosifier if required.

        2. ADD the material in stages e g. 5, 10, 15, 20 etc. ppb.

        3. STOP after desired concentration has been reached, or when it is observed that the material cannot be held in
       suspension

        4. If a higher LCM content is desired, LEAVE the pill to soak for a period of time and then TRY ADDING more
       material.

  - LCM Pill Spotting Procedure

   1. After spotting LCM pill, pull the bit above the calculated top of the pill. Wait 30minutes and monitor the annulus.
   2. After 30 minutes, attempt to fill the annulus, if not already full.  If the hole can be filled, close the BOP and squeeze
      10 bbls of LCM pill into the formation without exceeding 200 psi.
3.   If the hole remains stable, continue to drill ahead.
   4. Should the LCM pill fail to reduce the losses or the hole cannot be filled, then mix and spot another 50 bbls of LCM
         pill.
   5. Attempt to fill the annulus.  If successful, close the BOP and squeeze 20 bbls of LCM pill into the formation
       without exceeding 200 psi.  If a squeeze pressure develops, hold the pressure for 1 hour before staging the drill
        string back to bottom.

       Notes:

     -   All time hole must be full, transfer mud to trip tank and asked driller to fill hole from trip tank.

     -   All LCM pills must be thoroughly mixed and homogenised before being pumped.

     -  When pumping an LCM pill through a bit with nozzle smaller than 18/64, DO NOT USED LCM
      Medium and Coarse sizes.

     -   Remove filter screens from the drill pipe and if necessary from the mud pump suction lines.
     -   This is particularly true if the mud density is high, 14 lb/gal or greater. Fine loss circulation material which
        does not adsorb a lot of water should be used in weighted mud.

GUIDELINE FOR FIELD ENGINEERS FOR SHALE INHIBITION
      Shale Hydration takes two forms in water-sensitive shales: surface and osmotic adsorption. Surface hydration occurs
     when a small volume of water is strongly adsorbed onto the planar surfaces of the clays which causes little softening or
       swelling, but can lead to excessive stresses if the swelling is confined.
      Osmotic swelling occurs when a large volume of weakly held water is attracted to the clay surfaces by electrostatic
       forces. Osmotic swelling causes softening and significant swelling as the adjacent clay layers hydrate water and
      expand. Osmostic swelling does not generate excessive stresses, even when confined, and can be substantially reduced
         if Extremely difficult drilling conditions have led to more systematic approaches to analysing wellbore instabilities.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
          South Azadegan Oilfield Project

Contract No:          F – 20 Well Drilling Program                  Page 93 of 181

  One such approach that can be used as a guideline for the mud engineer involves three basic steps:
   1. Monitor and analyse the fundamental failure mechanism.
   2. Determine and understand the factors governing this failure mechanism.
   3. Model the problem considering impact of proposed changes.

  MONITOR AND ANALYZE THE FAILURE MECHANISM

   Monitoring of data and events on the rig is the first step of the process and involves:
    • Collection, organization and processing of drilling data. Drilling data such as Rate of Penetration (ROP),
   Revolutions per Minute (RPM), Weight-on-Bit (WOB), Bottom-Hole Assembly (BHA), torque, over pull Trip In Hole
   (TIH) and Pull Out Of Hole (POOH), swab and surge, flow rate, mud weight, mud Rehology , etc. should be collected,
   organized and analyzed on a continual basis and compared to the symptoms of wellbore stability being observed. This
   will help evaluate performance and establish trends or any deviations and measure the effectiveness of treatments and
   changes.
    • Formation characterization. The standard procedure in drilling operations is to apply the knowledge
   gained from previous wells to subsequent wells drilled in similar geologic regions. Wellbore-instability
   investigation requires evaluation of shales during the drilling process. Since rig site shale testing is limited
   due to equipment and time limitations, most evaluations are performed in a properly equipped laboratory.
   Laboratory tests are conducted to evaluate shale swelling, dispersibility, cation exchange capacity, hydration capacity,
   colloidal content, capillary suction time, shale hardness tests and mud compatibility properties.
  A number of methods have been used to perform rig site tests. These include tests as simple as the methylene blue test
  (CEC) measurement  of  shale;  shale  hardness measurements;  hot-rolled  dispersion  testing  with  various mud
   formulations and additives; and sophisticated analytical measurements of various clay properties. It is particularly
   important to try and determine the failure mechanism: swelling and softening or brittle spalling. Information from these
    tests, current shaker observations and mud logger
   data are used to evaluate drilling fluid performance, adjust mechanical drilling parameters and making casing design
   decisions. The primary options to counter shale problems at the rig site are:
    • Change the drilling fluid density.
    • Change to an oil- or synthetic-base system.
    • Change to a more inhibited water-base mud.
    • Reduce fluid loss to very low levels and increase filtrate viscosity.
    • Utilize pore-plugging additives, emulsified insoluble materials or plastering agents.
    • Adjust mechanical drilling parameters.
    • Evaluation and analysis of instability symptoms.
   Instability warning signs such as the presence of excessive cavings on the shakers for a given ROP, fill on connections,
   tripping difficulties, torque, stuck pipe, etc. indicate that something is wrong down hole. The magnitude or volume of
   failed rock should be estimated and recorded with the other drilling parameters. The number and location of specific
   incidents should be recorded, such as pipe
   sticking, fill or packing-off, reaming and re-drilling. All of this information can then be organized and correlated with
   time and depth to detect the location and quantify the severity of critical sections in the well.

   2. DETERMINE AND UNDERSTAND FAILURE MECHANISM
   All of the monitored and plotted parameters are then evaluated to identify the most probable mechanism causing the
    instability. The most useful symptoms used to indicate the mechanism responsible for wellbore instability are:
    • Cavings analysis.
    • Tripping difficulties.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
          South Azadegan Oilfield Project

Contract No:          F – 20 Well Drilling Program                  Page 94 of 181

    • Drilling conditions.
    • Mud system analysis.
    • In-situ conditions.
   These symptoms should be evaluated based on an interdisciplinary knowledge of the causes of wellbore
    instability to determine the most likely failure mechanism.

    3. MODEL AND CONSIDERING PROPOSED CHANGES
   Once the monitoring and determination steps have produced parameters, organize and analyze this data to obtain
    qualitative solutions and evaluate proposed changes. One good way to do this is through the use of a decision tree or
   flowchart approach to evaluate the possible failure mechanism and to identify the most promising remedies. After
    arriving at a proposed solution, a corrective measure should be applied and
    the results used as feedback into the method and decision tree method.
  A decision tree or flowchart, such as shown in Figure 9 for evaluating the cause of excessive cavings, provides a
    logical path for identification of the mechanism and a qualitative solution. Maintaining hole stability while drilling
    shale sections can be particularly troublesome. Simple solutions do not always exist, but good drilling practices
   combined with good mud practices are most often successful. Analysis of the mechanical stress/strain relationships has
   helped to develop the proper density requirements for hole stability.

5.5 Directional Drilling

  DRILLING FLUID PROPERTIES AND SELECTION
     It is common knowledge among drilling engineers and drillers that borehole problems can be considerably
   more severe in a directional hole than in a vertical well. The problems are magnified as the hole angle and
   horizontal displacement increases. Although most of these reactions are mechanical in nature, much can be
   done to alleviate them by proper selection and maintenance of the drilling fluid. For example, if a water-
   base fluid is being used, lubricants can be added to lower the coefficient of friction between the drill string
   and borehole or casing. Inhibiting salts can be added to reduce the interaction of the drilling fluid with the
   exposed formations, thereby improving borehole stability.

   There are a number of drilling factors influenced by the drilling fluid, but the following are of primary
   concern in drilling a horizontal well.
    • Borehole instability
    • Hole cleaning
    • Torque and drag
    • Formation damage
    • Barite sag
    • Hydraulics

   Borehole Instability
   Borehole instability is one of the more costly, but relatively common problem associated with
   drilling oil and gas wells. Not only can it impede drilling, it can result in loss of the hole. The term
   normally conjures thoughts of borehole collapse with accompanying stuck pipe. This is one of the

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
          South Azadegan Oilfield Project

Contract No:          F – 20 Well Drilling Program                  Page 95 of 181

  more critical reactions of borehole instability, but it can manifest itself in more subtle ways such as
   hole-fill, bridges, hole enlargement, or hole size reduction.
  These reactions lead to other associated problems including increased torque and drag, logging
   difficulties, poor cement jobs, and increased drilling days, all of which add significantly to the
   drilling cost. It should be pointed out that the discussions to follow do not cover hole enlargement
  problems from weakly consolidated sands, or rubble zones that erode from the hydraulic action of
   the drilling fluid.

   Stabilization
  The swelling activity is controlled by adjusting the electrolyte content of the fluid to minimize
  expansion of the clays. Lime and gypsum-treated fluids have long been used for this purpose, but
   basically do not satisfy the inhibition needs of the more troublesome shales. The potassium ion is
  an effective swelling inhibitor and is used extensively for this purpose through the addition of
  potassium salts. The concentration of potassium utilized ranges from several hundred mg/L to over
  60,000 mg/L. The most cost-effective potassium concentration, however, appears to be in the 3000
   to 7000 mg/L range. In marine environments, seawater or seawater with additional sodium
   chloride, are good alternates to potassium. The tendency for the shale to disperse or subdivide into
   discrete particles appears to be best controlled by the addition of an adsorptive, high molecular
  weight water-soluble polymer.
  One of the more effective polymers for this purpose is the partially hydrolyzed poly  acrylamide
  with molecular weight in excess of 15 million. These anionic polymers adsorb on to the surface of
   the cuttings and borehole wall to act as a binder or elastic coating to retard dispersion. Another
  approach to stabilizing the hard brittle shales that contain natural or induced micro fractures is the
  use of water-dispersible asphalt or asphaltines. The addition of these type materials appear to plug
   the micro-fractures, or perhaps even surface pore throats to retard further fluid invasion. This
   action has proven very effective in certain shales and can be applied in most water base fluids.

  Hole Cleaning
   Cleaning cuttings from a vertical hole has been covered extensively by numerous investigators. Hole cleaning in a
   vertical hole is essentially a matter of overcoming the slip velocity of the cuttings with sufficient annular velocity and
   carrying capacity of the drilling fluid. Although any velocity greater than the settling velocity of the largest cutting will
   theoretically lift all the cuttings to surface eventually, too low an annular velocity will lead to an undesirable high
   concentration of cuttings in the annulus. Experience has shown that cuttings concentrations more than 5% by volume
   cause tight hole or stuck pipe when circulation is stopped.

   Cuttings Bed Formation
   Radial slip velocity increases with increasing angle of inclination, forcing more cuttings to the low side of the annulus,
   which eventually results in the formation of a cuttings bed. reported that bed formation was dramatic in the 40° to 50°
   angle with the bed sliding downward against the flow, resulting in a very high cuttings concentration.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
          South Azadegan Oilfield Project

Contract No:          F – 20 Well Drilling Program                  Page 96 of 181

  The fact that a cutting bed forms is a serious problem, but the instability of the bed in this critical angle of the well
  makes the problem even more severe. High torque and drag resulting from the high cutting concentration at the lower
   part of the angle can obviously result in stuck pipe or loss of circulation.

  Hole Cleaning Sweeps
   In static drill string concluded that high-density sweeps were more effective than high-density high-viscosity sweeps
   based on field measurements. The higher viscosity of the latter caused flow diversion to the high side of the wellbore.
  Not surprisingly, high-viscosity sweeps also performed poorly. Also concluded that the best hole cleaning sweeps were
   high-density low viscosity sweeps, with a density 3 – 4 ppg higher than that of the drilling fluid.  the following
   procedure is recommended:
    • Calculate sweep height in the vertical part of the annulus, and added hydrostatic head in the annulus.
    • Rotate at 80+ rpm while pumping the sweep, 180+ rpm is preferred. It is not recommended to pump a weighted
   sweep while sliding.
    • Keep pumping until the sweep is out of the hole. If pumping stops while the sweep is in the
   deviated hole, it falls out of the “jet stream” and re-deposits captured fines.
    • Avoid increasing the viscosity of the weighted sweep.
    • Plan to run 3 – 4 sweeps to achieve desired results when hole cleaning problems are
   indicated. One sweep will probably not be sufficient to remedy an existing situation.
    • Avoid checking for flow or closing the annulus with the weighted sweep in the drill pipe.
    • If desired for preventative maintenance: 1) schedule sweeps every 6 – 8 hours or 500 –
  1000 feet (whichever comes first, unless the hole dictates greater frequency); barite/silt
   build- ups are dependent on time and footage: and 2) pump a sweep while circulating
   bottom up before tripping, and another once back on bottom.
   concluded that high-density sweeps were more effective than high-viscosity sweeps.
  They strongly advocated the use of sweep reports, which should be standard practice in any drilling fluids operation.

   Short Tripping
   Another technique that has proven helpful in controlling problems created by the cuttings bed in the high-angle portion
   of the well is to make periodic short trips. Tripping the bit through the bed not only assures that the bottom hole
   assembly and bit can be pulled through it, but the action should agitate the bed to the point that a portion will slide
  down the hole and subsequently be picked up when circulation is commenced. The time intervals for these short trips
   will be determined by the torque and drag conditions that exist in any particular well. Lacking specific well history, a
   general rule of thumb is to short trip at least once for every 500 feet of new hole.

   Drill String Rotation
  The industry is in total agreement that drill string rotation improves hole cleaning, especially in hole sizes less than 17
   ½”. The general opinion in the industry is that the optimum drill string rotation rate is approximately 120 rpm. It is
   believed that the drill string rotation improves hole cleaning by mechanically disturbing the cuttings bed on the low
   side of the hole. There could also be effects due to skewing the velocity profile in the wellbore and the creation of
   Taylor Vortices. Encouraged the use of rotary steerable systems.

   Turbulence

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
          South Azadegan Oilfield Project

Contract No:          F – 20 Well Drilling Program                  Page 97 of 181

  Bottom hole assembly design becomes important because the flow restrictions of some of the components, such as the
   measurement-while-drilling tools or positive displacement motors, may not allow sufficient flow to reach turbulence in
   the annulus. Turbulence can, of course, be induced at some constant flow rate by decreasing the annular area. This may
   be more difficult to accomplish in a horizontal well because of limitations on the design of drill strings and tool
   assemblies. An increase in the effective viscosity of the drilling fluid will decrease the Reynolds Number, and thus
   increase the flow rate required to obtain turbulent flow. Thus, a lower effective viscosity fluid may be desirable in the
   horizontal well, but attention must also be given to cleaning the larger diameter portion of the upper hole or possible
   washed-out sections where it is impractical to maintain turbulent flow.
    It is interesting to point out a basic fact between static fluid pressure and dynamic fluid pressure. Static fluid pressure is
   a function of true vertical depth, while dynamic fluid pressure, or ECD, is a function of measured depth. Thus, the
  ECD will continue to increase, albeit slowly, even after the hole becomes essentially horizontal. The fracture pressure
   could be reached if the hole was extended to sufficient depth and the fluid density is close to the fracture gradient.

  Hole Cleaning Guidelines
  The single most important factor related to hole cleaning in deviated wells is flow rate. During directional drilling
   operations drilled cuttings will settle on the low side of the hole and form a stationary bed if insufficient annular fluid
   velocity is used. The critical flow rate required to prevent cuttings bed formation must be determined .
   Typically few hole cleaning problems exist in vertical or horizontal sections. Most problems
   associated with hole cleaning are seen on deviated wells and occur in the 40 – 60 degree section where gravity effects
   can cause cuttings beds to slump down the hole.Typical flow rates to aim for in ERD wells:

         Hole
                                               Typical Flow Rates
        Size
           17 ½”      800 gpm minimum. Some rigs achieve 850 – 1000 gpm.

          12 ¼”    Aim for 800 gpm (although 800 – 1000 gpm is typically achieved). If 800
                 gpm is not achievable, ensure tripping procedures are in place for poorly
                      cleaned hole.

           8 ½”                       Aim for 400 gpm.

  When correctly designed, both laminar and turbulent flow regimes will effectively clean a deviated well. In general
   increasing the viscosity of a fluid in laminar flow will improve hole cleaning as will a reduction of the viscosity of a
   fluid in turbulent flow. It is important that one or other regime is selected and that the transition zone between the two
    is avoided.
   Generally viscous fluids in laminar flow are preferred because:
    • It is possible to achieve higher cleaning capacity.
    • Viscous fluids give better transport in the near-vertical sections.
    • Viscous fluids have better suspension characteristics when circulation is stopped.
    • It is difficult to achieve “turbulent flow” except in small hole sizes.
   Experience has shown that good drilling fluid rheological properties are extremely important to hole cleaning when
   drilling a high angle well. Studies show that the effects of drilling fluid

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
          South Azadegan Oilfield Project

Contract No:          F – 20 Well Drilling Program                  Page 98 of 181

   rheological properties and annular flow regime are mutually dependent. In the laminar flow regime, increasing fluid
   viscosity will improve hole cleaning and this is particularly effective if the Yield Stress is high. In the turbulent regime,
   however, reducing fluid viscosity will help removing cuttings. Therefore the drilling fluid rheological properties should
   be designed to avoid the transitional flow regime. For hole sizes above 8 ½”, the annular flow is laminar under most
   circumstances. Therefore  it is necessary to specify a minimum Yield Stress. The optimum level required is best
   established based on field data and experience.

  A higher ROP requires a higher flow rate to clean the hole. It is good practice to drill the hole with a steady ROP and
   select the required flow  rate  for hole cleaning accordingly. Before making a connection, the hole should be
   circulated at the normal flow rate to clear the cuttings from around the BHA. Depending upon the hole angle and
   length of BHA, a circulation time of 5 to 10 minutes is often required.

   Before tripping out, the hole should be circulated at the normal flow rate until the shakers are clean, and the drill pipe
   be rotated at maximum speed/reciprocated in the meantime. This may require up to 3 bottoms-ups, depending on the
   hole angle and hole size. Below are listed the recommended number of bottoms-up prior to tripping.

             Hole Angle, deg       8 ½”          12 ¼”         17 ½”

                 0 – 10               1.3              1.3              1.5

                10 – 30              1.4              1.4              1.7

                30 – 60              1.6              1.8              2.5

                  60+                1.7              2.0              3.0

  Torque and Drag
   Overall friction can become so great in an extended-reach well or horizontal section that the drill string can only be
   rotated with great difficulty. Needless to say, this condition is conducive to stuck pipe.  Obviously,  this  coefficient
   of  friction will be  significantly affected by the lubricating properties of the drilling fluid.

  Many of the causes of torque and drag are mechanical, while others are related to hole geometry. Several, however,
   can be directly attributed to the drilling fluid. Since wall contact with the drill string will be extremely high in a
   horizontal hole, it is imperative that the lubricating characteristics
   of the fluid be considered.
  Formation Damage
  The obvious reason for drilling a horizontal well is to expose more of the producing zone to the wellbore for increased
   production. Thus, the importance of preventing formation damage during drilling and completion of the horizontal well
    is just as important as it is in its vertical counterpart.
   In fact,  it may be more important since the increased cost of drilling the horizontal well must show additional
   production for its justification. A 50% damage or permeability loss in a potential 500 bbl/day horizontal well represents
   a considerably greater loss in production than a 50% damage in a 100 bbl/day vertical hole through the same reservoir.
   Sandstone production is by far the most susceptible reservoir rock to damage or permeability loss during drilling and
   completion operations. Though formation damage may be caused by poor drilling and completion practices, one of the
   primary causes is due to exposure to the drilling and completion fluid.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
          South Azadegan Oilfield Project

Contract No:          F – 20 Well Drilling Program                  Page 99 of 181

   This loss, attributable to the fluid, can be caused by one or more of the following occurrences:
    • Particle transport from the drilling fluid into the production zone, subsequently plugging pore throats.
    • Fluid filtrate reacting with expandable clays in the rock to decrease pore throat diameters.
    • Particle movement within the permeable rock due to dispersion of clays and other minerals from quartz surfaces.
    • Wettability changes of the formation from exposure to the drilling fluid filtrate.
    • Interaction of fluid filtrate with formation fluids to form water - insoluble precipitants.
   The effect of these actions on the permeability of sandstones has been the subject of a considerable investigation. In
   almost all cases, some damage is caused by the drilling and completion fluid. The primary objective is to limit this
   damage as much as possible. Consequently, selection of the drilling and completion fluid becomes an extremely
   important function in drilling the horizontal well.

   Recommended Fluid Parameters

    • Velocity – One of the major obstacles to overcome in horizontal and ERD drilling is hole cleaning. In vertical holes,
    cuttings have room to settle without causing problems. In horizontal wells, cuttings only have to settle 4+ inches to
   form a cuttings bed. Research and practical experience has shown that cuttings bed formation is analogous to sand
   dunes on an ocean beach. The bed moves in a wave fashion up the hole. If sufficient annular velocity is
   not present, the bed thickens until it reaches equilibrium, which usually occurs when turbulent flow conditions exist.
   With water, turbulent flow is easily obtained since viscosity is in the denominator of the turbulent flow equation, and
   water has a viscosity of 1. Fluids other than water require more pump output to approach turbulent flow conditions.
    Practical
   experience in other industries has proven that materials can be transported in water slurries if the fluid velocity is
   between 3 and 7 ft/sec.

    • Rheological Properties – Ideally one would want a true thixotropic fluid to drill horizontally.
    In effect, two different conditions exist simultaneously. One occurs in the vertical portion of
    the hole where larger diameter casing may be set, or wash-outs in the open hole occur. In that case, a fluid having a
   high Yield Stress, or if the fluid is visco-elastic, the initial gel strength needs to be elevated. The opposite is needed in
    the horizontal section from a hole-cleaning point of view. Here one wants water for the reasons pointed out earlier.
   Compromises are the result. If casing is set into the zone of interest, or if formations up-hole are not a problem, then
   water may be possibly combined with occasional sweeps.

    • Surface Apparatus – Special attention must be placed on the solids removal equipment at the surface. Solids removal
    efficiency must be continually monitored so that unwanted particulate material is not retained in the fluid system that
   may negatively affect fluid weight
    or filter cake quality, resulting in lost circulation or increased torque and drag. The cost of problems that may occur due
    to not doing this correctly may far outweigh the cost of equipment needed.

5.6 Hydrogen Sulphide Gas in Drilling Fluid

   Description of Hydrogen Sulphide Gas
   Hydrogen sulphide is a colourless gas, with an offensive odour and a sweetish taste. It is soluble in water, alcohol, oils,
   and many other solvents. It has a specific gravity of 1.1895 with reference to air. It is considered a weak acid, it is toxic
    to humans and corrosive to metals. Hydrogen sulphide can be dangerous to personnel on the surface as it is extremely
    toxic to human and even animal life, and is extremely corrosive to most metals as it can cause cracking of drill pipe
   and tubular goods, and destruction of testing tools and wire lines. When hydrogen sulphide enters the borehole during

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
          South Azadegan Oilfield Project

Contract No:          F – 20 Well Drilling Program                  Page 100 of 181

    drilling, completion, or testing for hydrocarbons,  it creates several very detrimental problems. These problems are
   encountered regardless of the source of the hydrogen sulphide. Hydrogen sulphide has created unsolved problems in
   the oil field.

   Origin of Hydrogen Sulphide

   During oil and gas operations, hydrogen sulphide can be found as a component of formation gases, dissolved in water,
   hydrocarbons, or even liquid sulphur. Thermal degradation of organic materials and sulphate reducing bacteria (SRB)
   can create hydrogen sulphide along with other gases.

  Problems Caused By Hydrogen Sulphide Gas Health and Safety of the Personnel

  One of the main problems hydrogen sulphide causes is in respect to the health and safety and safety of the personnel. A
  maximum of eight hours exposure to concentrations greater than 100 ppm H2S will cause hemorrhage and death.
   Concentrations above 600 ppm can be fatal in three to five minutes. Highly stressed, high strength steel can fail in a
   matter of minutes in the presence of 50 ppm H2S. At high pressure, as little as 0.1 ppm H2S can greatly reduce the time
   to failure of highly stressed, high strength steel. Relying solely on its odour is not a good idea because at concentrations
   above 100 ppm it deadens a person’s sense of smell within a few minutes. The pure gas is heavier than air and can
   collect in low areas such as pit rooms and accommodation. below Table shows the potential hazards hydrogen sulphide
   can cause to the personnel from short-term exposures. There are many historical cases to document the ill effects of
   hydrogen sulphide exposure.
  The documented cases reported in Table 2 indicate that hydrogen sulphide is very toxic and it takes only a matter of
   minutes to affect unprotected workers.

                         Health effects from short-term exposure to hydrogen sulphide

               Concentration
                                                           Health Effect
                 (ppm)
                   0.01 – 0.3            Odour threshold
                                          Offensive odour, possible nausea, tearing of the eyes or                  1 – 20
                                        headaches with prolonged exposure
                                        Nose, throat and long irritation; digestive upset and loss of
                  20 – 50                 appetite; sense of smell starts to become fatigued; acute
                                               conjunctivitis may occur (pain, tearing and light sensitivity)
                                         Severe nose, throat and long irritation; ability to smell odour                 100 – 200
                                         completely disappears.
                                         Severe  lung   irritation,  excitement,  headache,   dizziness,
                                              staggering, sudden collapse knockdown), unconsciousness and
                   500                 death within a few hours, loss of memory for the period of
                                         exposure (results in permanent brain damage  if not rescued
                                           immediately).
                500 – 1000             Respiratory paralysis, irregular heart beat, collapse and

                                          death without rescue.
                > 1000              Rapid collapse and death

   Effects of hydrogen sulphide on Metals
   Hydrogen sulphide mainly cause corrosion problems to drill strings, transport pipes, storage tanks etc. Hydrogen
   sulphide causes sulphide stress cracking, hydrogen embrittlement and pitting corrosion in oil and gas operations. The
   corrosion of iron in the presence of hydrogen sulphide and water is dependent upon the dissociation of the hydrogen-
   sulphide molecule. Iron is oxidized to the ferrous form at the anode and hydrogen sulphide under- goes a two-step
   dissociation at the cathode. Tung et al22propose the following equilibrium reactions below.
   At the anode:
   Fe        Fe2+ + 2e-
  And at the cathode hydrogen is produced and either enters the steel or forms hydrogen gas and bubbles off:
    If H2S is present:
  2H+ + 2e- H° + H°
    If H2S is not present:
  H° + H°         H2
   Hydrogen enters the steel first by adsorption onto the water to steel interface and then by being absorbed into the steel
   as hydrogen. The amount of hydrogen entry depends on the corrosion rate of the steel surface and the concentration of
   anions such as HS- that reduce the tendency to produce hydrogen gas (as shown in second equation above) and
   promotes the hydrogen (Ho) to enter the steel. pH also has an influence on amount of corrosion damage. Basically, the
   term “sulphides” used in oil and gas operations includes all three water-soluble species, H2S, HS- and S2-, which
   coexist in a sulphide-water system. Shown in the ionization chart for hydrogen sulphide in figure 1, it can be observed
   that molecular H2S predominates in the acidic range, where the pH is below 7. In the range of 7 to 12, monovalent HS-
   predominates and above pH 12, divalent S2- predominates.
  The illustration in figure 1 indicates that the most important practice is that when H2S enters in alkaline mud, it reacts
   to form an alkaline sulphide, most often sodium sulphide.17 While elevated pH is a good way to keep H2S from
   causing problems under certain conditions,  it does not remove  it from the fluid and any drop in pH can create a
   significant hazard. This then brought about the advent of sulphide scavengers in the history of oil and gas operations.
  The only safe method for the total removal of hydrogen sulphide or soluble sulphides is with a sulphide scavenger.

   Effects of hydrogen sulphide on Drilling fluid

  The first positive identification of soluble sulphide in the drilling fluids must be taken as a possible indication of H2S
   entering the fluid from some source. In the simplest case an increase in mud weight may be sufficient to control minor
   influxes. If levels persist chemical treatment will be required. This can take two forms; neutralization and scavenging.
  The two terms must not be confused. Neutralization of H2S in drilling fluid by caustic soda or lime is a common field
   approach. This alkalinity maintenance method has proved successful when small amounts of H2S enter the drilling
    fluid, for example, from drilling up pore spaces containing the gas.
   This is not a suitable approach to the control on influxes of Hydrogen Sulphide. The alkalinity neutralization of H2S is
   instantly reversible by reduction in pH. Should the pH of the fluid decrease due to further influxes of H2S, Co2 or
   brine flows the sulphids may come out of solution as hydrogen sulphide gas. To ensure that such influxes are handled
   safely, it is essential to employ a sulphide scavenger. This term refers to any additive which is capable of reacting with
   one or more of the sulphide species and as a result, converting them to a more inert form. To be truly effective an
   irreversible and complete reaction must occur. As a mutual equilibrium exist between the three sulphide species in
   solution (H2S, HS‐ and S2‐) irreversible and complete removal of one serves to removal all three.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
          South Azadegan Oilfield Project

Contract No:          F – 20 Well Drilling Program                  Page 102 of 181

  To remove the problem it is necessary to precipitate the sulphide as an insoluble inorganic sulphide. Zinc or iron
  compounds are usually chosen because of the relative inertness of the sulphids formed. Zinc source (Zinc oxide or
   carbonate) has in the past been the preferred treatment. However, continuous treatment with Zinc carbonate will
   produce significant undesirable carbonate concentration (Carbonate contamination) which can adversely effect on
   drilling fluid rehology and fluid loss. See the below table:

    Contaminant   FV   PV   YP    Gels   FL   pH   Pm     Pf   Mf    Cl‐   Ca2+

     Carbonate               _                                                    _

      H2S                  _                                                    _
                        Quick reference for recognizing carbonate and H2S contamination

  As a precaution against Hydrogen Sulphide influxes into the drilling fluid system should be pre‐ treated with
   Zinc  Source. Zinc  oxide can be used  as an  alternative, provided  that  the  possible environmental payloads
   associated with  its use are appreciated. pH and alkalinity tests (Pf/Mf) are a good  first indicator of Hydrogen
   Sulphide influx. However, the use of scavenger is used up. It can be seen from this that while a scavenger increase
   safety  levels,  it also makes detection of small Hydrogen Sulphide influxes difficult by conventional methods.
   Measurement of the concentration of scavenger in the mud system is possible but the analysis method is not safe for
   use on the rig.

  The most effective environmentally acceptable scavengers for Hydrogen Sulphide in water base muds are Zinc
   sources like Zinc Carbonate or Oxide, primarily because of their solubility which leads to very fast reaction rates
   compared to other scavengers.

   Zinc Sources clearly reacts very much faster than the other scavengers. This is due to the fact that the  iron  in
   solution ensures a fast reaction with the sulphides and the chemistry in this case produces elemental sulphur as
   the reaction product. Elemental sulphur is completely insoluble in water. The iron is reduced from Fe+3 to Fe+2 in
   the process and some of this will be regenerated to produce Fe+3 when the mud comes in to contact with the air.
  Any regeneration of Fe+3 will aid in reducing product consumption without affecting is scavenging properties.
  Recommended treatment levels of Zinc sources are as follows:
   pre‐ emptive treatment is recommended at 1.0 lb/bbl treatment of a mud system in the event of gas influx should be
  made on the basis that 2.0 lb/bb; will treat approximately 100 ppm Hydrogen Sulphide.

   Testing / Reporting/ Treatment for Hydrogen Sulphide

  The active mud system must be regularly analyzed and monitored for the presence of active soluble Sulphide with the
   Garrett Gas train. This test must be carried out at the same time as each complete mud check, i.e.: three times per day
   while drilling.
   Alkalinity  checks  should  be  carried  out  intermittently  in  between  these  full  checks  and  any significant
   reduction in the pH and Mf/Pf should be followed up by testing for the presence of active soluble sulphides with the
   Garrett Gas Train.
   For routine checks lead acetate paper can be used to give a qualitative check. Should this test be positive then it must
   be followed by a qualitative analysis with a Dragger tube.
  The following actions must be taken after the initial positive test result:

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
           South Azadegan Oilfield Project

   Contract No:          F – 20 Well Drilling Program                  Page 103 of 181

        1. Inform the Drilling Supervisor
        2. Start adding H2S Scavenger to the active mud system. Active soluble sulphides can be analysed and monitored
      because of their characteristic of readily regenerating hydrogen Sulphide gas in reaction with mild acid. By adding
        citric acid to whole mud sample in a Garrett gas train detection of Hydrogen Sulphide on lead acetate paper indicates
       the presence of active Sulphides in the mud. When this qualitative test proves positive a dragger tube should be used to
       give qualitative results.
     However it must be remembered that pre‐treating the mud with MILGARD Fe means that no
      Sulphides will be delectable until all the scavenger has reacted,  i.e.:  it is impossible to detect small influxes of
      Hydrogen Sulphide in a system pre‐ treating with MILGARD Fe .

      Contingency stock to be kept on hand on the rig

     The following Contingency stock of treatment chemicals must be kept on hand on the rig over and above the normal
     mud maintenance material requirements.
      Zinc Carbonate or Oxide 120 Sxs 25 kg
     Lime 200 Sxs 25 kg
     As above mentioned Hydrogen Sulphide is a poisonous and dangerous acidic gas encountered in many formations. It
      can quickly deaden senses and can be fatal, even at low concentration. Hydrogen Sulphide is characterized by its
       typical “rotten egg” smell. For safety reasons,  it should be neutralized immediately with caustic soda or lime to
       increase pH to >11.5 to form sulphide (S2‐), then precipitated with a source of Zinc.
       Neutralization with caustic soda:
      2 NaOH + H2S 2Na+ + S2‐ + 2H2O (at pH>11.5)
       Neutralization with maintaining excess lime:
      2 Ca(OH)2 + H2S Ca2+ + S2‐ + 2H2O (at pH>11.5)
      Converting Hydrogen Sulphide into just sulphide by increasing pH is not a permanent reaction. If the pH were drop
       into the acid region, sulphide will be converting back into the poisonous H2S form. For this reasons H2S should
      always be precipitated by adding a source of Zinc such as Zinc Oxide.
      Removal by precipitation with treatments of (ZnO):
     H2S + ZnO ZnS + H2O
         if H2S gas detected in mud following action should be taken to:
        4. Immediately rise up the pH to at least 11.5 to 12 with caustic soda.
        5. Buffer the pH with lime.
        6. Begin treatment with Zinc oxide to remove sulphide (S2‐) from the system. (1 lb/bbl treatment of Zinc oxide
      removes approximately 1100 mg/lit of sulphide. No more than 2 lb/bbl of Zinc oxide should be used to pre‐treat the
      system for H2S)
         If it appears that H2S bleeding in (flowing) form the formation, the mud density should be
       increased to shut off the flow of gas into the well‐bore.

Hazard and risk assessment

The use of chemicals in the workplace presents hazards and risks to personnel involved in their handling and application. In
order to minimize these hazards risk assessments are performed and HSE control measures and management systems are
established to achieve the following:-

     Identify hazards
       Safely manage those hazards.

     Identify risks
       Where possible, eliminate those risks through control / engineering measures e.g. ventilation and collection of dust.

       Where not possible, manage those risks through processes  including the use of PPE.

       Provide training and awareness systems designed to achieve the above and promote continuous improvement.

Hazardous materials

       The effect on a person of a hazardous material depends on:
       The nature of the hazardous material.
       The site of the action.
       The amount of the hazardous material involved (“dose”).
       The reaction of the individual (“susceptibility”).

Hazardous Material Effects

Local effects:
       Skin and eye irritation & burns.
       Skin defatting leading to dermatitis.

Systemic effects:
       Central nervous system (headaches, nausea, dizziness).
       Cardiovascular system (CO poisoning).
       Sensitisation (allergy) and asthma.
       Teratogenic and carcinogenic.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
           South Azadegan Oilfield Project

   Contract No:          F – 20 Well Drilling Program                  Page 105 of 181

Chemical hazardous effects may be :
       Acute - effects lasting minutes, hours or days e.g. irritation i.e. generally short term recoverable effects.
       Chronic - effects lasting weeks, months or years e.g. occupational asthma – generally long and possibly permanent
          effects.
       Effects may be reversible or irreversible.

6.  Waste Management Program

  6.1 Drilling Waste Management Design

The contractor is responsible for designing the optimum practices for treating the produced waste during the
operation. The initial technical waste management proposal should be included the practices for minimizing,
treating and recovering of the wastes.
According to the below categories:
        .  Water based mud containing minerals and natural synthetic polymers, and
        .   Cuttings contaminated with WBM.

The contractor is encouraged to offer possible practices to minimize the drilling fluids waste and its regeneration.

 6.1.1 Water Based mud containing minerals, naturals and synthetic polymers

        .  Primary operation

             The proposed primary solids control package should be able to control drilling fluid properties, reducing of
        mud materials usage against conventional solid control equipment, and significantly reduce the number of excess
             fluids generated throughout drilling each phase of the well.

        .  Secondary Operation

                All excess mud and spillage (water based) should be collected in rig site pits and free surface fluid should be
             treated using a built for propose flocculation station (has to join with centrifuge) consisting of integral dosing and
         pumps along with a centrifuge for efficient, quick and controlled separation of the solids before the cleaned fluids
               is returned to active system or buffer tanks.
               This system should be able to remove colloidal sized particles that cannot be removed with primary solids
            control system.
               This system may use to clean up the remaining water-based mud or decrease weight of oil base mud at the
          end of the drilling fluids programmed.

 6.1.2 Cuttings Contaminated with WBM

Spud mud and water-based mud cuttings should be fixed and be ready for transport to disposal zone. For fixing the cutting,
100-150 kg cement shall be used for each cubic meters of cutting as well as 3-4-liter Sodium Silicate for each cubic meter of
cutting. (Consumption of cement and sodium silicate varies in different formations). Gypsum is not allowed for cutting
fixation. This method is physically Bonds Contaminants to Prevent Leaching to Environment. Final product will pass can test
and in case of approval by company man it’ll send to landfill.

  6.2 Waste Discharge and Handling

The Company is responsible for designing of required pits and The Contractor is responsible for procedure for
burring of waste cuttings in disposal zone; supervision and managing of requirement operation for deposit oil
waste cutting.
The contractor is responsible for treating all the generated waste in site without any discharges in environment or
damages. It is also the responsibility of the contractor for handling/discharge of generated waste after treatment.

  6.8 Waste Management Policy

Waste management services should be utilized to minimize generated waste and contaminated discharged
cuttings during drilling operations. This helps keep the environment clean and ensures that our operations are in
compliance with DOE (Department of Environment) legislations.
We provide cost-effective and optimum solutions from environmentally acceptable procedures to achieve zero-
discharge plans and improve fluids recovery. The outcome reduces operational costs and enables recycling.
The waste is analysed to study various parameters such as chloride, nitrate, and phosphate. Other analysis is
carried out to monitor treatment processes on a regular basis during waste generation. Our waste management
personnel are fully trained experienced and have the competencies in place to provide the best reliable services
with concentrated support and the least liabilities.

      6.10.2.4 High Performance Filter Unit
Filtration plays an important role in the natural treatment of waste water as it percolates through the soil. It is also a major part
of most water treatment. Groundwater that has been softened, or treated through iron and manganese oxidation, requires
filtration to remove floc created by coagulation or oxidation processes. Since surface water is subject to run-off and does not
undergo natural filtration.
it must be filtered to remove particles and impurities. Filtration can be compared to a sieve or micro- strainer that traps
suspended material between the grains of filter media. However, since most suspended particles can easily pass through the
spaces between grains of the filter media, straining is the least important process in filtration. Filtration primarily depends on a
combination of complex physical and chemical mechanisms, the most important being adsorption. Adsorption is the process
of particles sticking onto the surface of the individual filter grains or onto the previously deposited materials. Forces that

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
           South Azadegan Oilfield Project

   Contract No:          F – 20 Well Drilling Program                  Page 115 of 181

attract and hold particles to the grains are the same as those that work in coagulation and flocculation. In fact, coagulation and
flocculation may occur in the filter bed, especially if coagulation and flocculation before filtration was not properly controlled.
Incomplete coagulation can cause serious problems in filter operation.

7.  Cement Program

Summary:
      Cement of the well is part of the well integrity barrier envelope. In addition, cement protects the casing from corrosion due to
         potential formation fluids on the outside well.
      Cementing in the 17 ½” section returns to surface has historically been a challenge. This is because of the formation strength

        below the 20” surface casing is not sufficient to bring cement all them way to the surface.
      Cementing the 12 ¼” section is as equally challenging as for the 17 ½” section, due to small limit between pore pressure and
         fracture pressure.
       20” surface casing will be set @±150m. Cemented up to surface till return to surface (cellar floor).
      13 ⅜" intermediate casing will be set @±2182m. Cemented up to cellar floor. 100 % excess on slurries for open hole-Casing
       volume & 20% excess on slurries for casing-casing volume.
      9 ⅝" production casing will be set @±3915m. It will be cemented up to cellar floor. 100% excess on slurries for open hole-
        Casing volume & 20% excess on slurries for casing-casing volume.
       7" liner will be set @ ±4199 mMD. It will be cemented up to liner lap @ ±3862.5 m (80 m overlap on production CSG). 100%
        excess on slurries for open Hole-Casing volume & 20% excess on slurries for casing-casing volume.

General Procedure:

          Prior to each cement job, the unit will be checked accurately. Fluid ends, engines, etc.

        The lines will have to be flushed with the mixing water. Cementing heads, plugs will be inspected before the job.

        The plugs will be inserted into the cementing head in presence of the Drilling Operation Representative.

        Bumping the plug is MUST.

        The cement density will be checked, using pressurized balance.

        Volume calculations & displacement will be done by & cement engineer.

         Rig pump should be used to displace the cement.

Important: The following program is a sample; therefore,  it should be tested using the field drilling water, cement sample and

additives.

  9.6 Material Selection

Casing
Corrosive Environment Analysis and Strength Check
Based on the data of offset wells in this oilfield, there is a certain degree of corrosive problems in the 4 layers to be produced.
Corrosive medium is principally composed of 2 parts: one is the corrosive gases like H2S and CO2 existing in the produced fluid; and
the other is the high salinity formation water. The contents of main corrosive fluids in offset wells are shown in Table.

     1)   Corrosion in this oilfield is a result of combined action of H2S, CO2 and high salinity formation water. The corrosive
        mechanism is complicated, and the corrosive rate on the casing is difficult to determine. Based on the NACE standard and
          the data from the Table, the producers result in severe corrosive environment. However, by the research on development
          conditions and technical indices,  it can be found that although the corrosive fluid contents are high in the oilfield, the
          corrosive environment is not extremely serious as the following reasons:

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
           South Azadegan Oilfield Project

   Contract No:          F – 20 Well Drilling Program                  Page 137 of 181

     2)   In general, the temperature that cause the SSC/SCC is concentrated in the range from 20℃ to 30℃. The average depth of
          the horizontal section is 2,730m with the reservoir temperature of 96℃, and tensional stress on the casing does not exist in
          the horizontal section. Therefore, the influence of SSC/SCC may not be considered for the casing/liner.

     3)  Based on the RMDP, the production GOR in the  first development phase should not be higher than the  initial GOR,
          therefore, the flowing pressure is higher than the bubble point pressure all the time, that is, degassing problems do not exist
           in the horizontal section and some vertical section, the H2S and CO2 have no chance of separation out of oil phase, and it
            is lack of prerequisite for casing corrosion.

     4)  Based on above all, the corrosive environment of the casing string is not severe. The optimal selection for the casing string
        can be stated briefly as follows

Material Selection of Casing String

             2)   In this oilfield, the corrosion of production string obviously occurs in 2 regions. One is the region where
                 the pressure is above the bubble point; in which degassing problems do not exist, and the corrosion
                environment is relatively weak. Another one is the region where the pressure is below the bubble point;

               because of the decline of pressure, the concentration of corrosive gas will increase and the corrosion is
                    relatively serious. Considering the corrosive environment is not serious in the low water cut production
                stage and the production practice in this oilfield has proved the L80 is fit for it, So, the L80 should be
                taken as the material of 9-5/8″ casing, 7″, 5 1/2″ and 4-1/2″ liner. In order to prevent the hazard of gas
                leakage after production degassing occurs, 9-5/8″ casing, 7″ and 4-1/2″ liner should be Gas tight
                  thread. 2) The 110S is selected as the material of 9-5/8″/7″/4-1/2″ casing rather than L-80 due to the
                 high strength requirement in Fahliyan wells, what’s more the 110S is fit for sour gas environment.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
            South Azadegan Oilfield Project

     Contract No:          F – 20 Well Drilling Program                  Page 138 of 181

Selection of PR (Performance Requirement)
According to API Specification 6A 19th edition, performance requirements are specific nd unique to the product
in the as-shipped condition. All products shall be designed to perform according to the requirements of API 6A
clauses 4 and clauses 10 while in the pressure and temperature ranges and corrosion envirement. Other
requirements include load capability, cycles, and operating force or torque. There are two performance
requirement levels: PR1 and PR2. Valves operating as safety valves shall be of the PR2 performance level and
meet the requirements of Annex I in API 6A For SA wells, the guideline for PR selection is that all parts not
contacting well fluids in nomal production period are PR2, all parts contacting well fluids are PR2 as follow,
Casing head housing and casing head spool assembly – PR2. Tubing head spool, tubing hanger and X-mas
tree - PR2.
Temperature Class

Environment temp:
Well temp: 0.0244×4100+27.384=127℃

12. HSE & H2S Safety plan & Well Control

  12.1 General Quality, Health, Safety & Environmental Requirements

     PPL/PEDEC’s committed to conducting operations in a manner which minimizes the risks to
          the health and safety of those involved in, and affected by, such operations. PPL/PEDEC’s also
         committed to conducting its operations in a manner which minimizes the impact on natural and
            ‘built’ environments in the area of operations.
     This section sets out minimum Quality, Health, Safety and Environmental (QHSE) requirements
           for the WORK that MSA and subcontractors will be required to comply with whilst conducting
          operations for PPL/PEDEC.
     PPL/PEDEC may perform audits and assessments of MSA’s and its subcontractors’
         performance under the CONTRACT. PPL/PEDEC reserves the right without prior notice to
         conduct quality, health, safety, security and environmental audits of the operations.
     MSA shall at all times be responsible for performing the WORK in a manner so as to protect
          against loss of life, injury to people, damage to property and the environment. The detailed
        manner means and methods of performing the WORK shall be under the control and direction
          of MSA.
     Prior to the start of Operations, certain QHSE processes must be completed. These QHSE
          processes include developing the final QHSE plan format and frequency of any QHSE audits,
          permitting and licensing and PEDEC’s approvals. MSA shall ensure that sufficient resources are
          dedicated to this task to ensure completion prior to mobilization.
     PPL/PEDEC may at any time stop the WORK or any portion of the WORK without penalty to
       PPL/PEDE Cif the WORK is not conducted in accordance with the Quality, Health, Safety or
         Environmental guidelines specified in this CONTRACT or by the laws of the appropriate
           jurisdiction. PPL/PEDEC reserves the right to require MSA to remove any person from the
          operations in the case of non-compliance with QHSE procedures. PPL/PEDEC may also
          prohibit the use of equipment and plant in cases where that equipment or plant does not meet the
          required standards or is deemed unsafe.
     MSA shall ensure that activities associated with this CONTRACT are conducted in compliance
         with applicable national and local HSE laws and regulations, MSA’s standards and PEDEC’s
          requirements and IADC documents.
     Limits of Authority
     PEDEC’s Representative(s) do not have the authority to relax health, safety or environment
          standards. If they judge the operation to be inadequate with regards to any aspect of quality,
           health, safety, security or the environment, then the operation may be immediately suspended.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
           South Azadegan Oilfield Project

   Contract No:          F – 20 Well Drilling Program                  Page 151 of 181

    MSA shall be required to develop a project-specific QHSE plan to manage the WORK and
         associated risks. This plan shall, as a minimum, address topics included in the list detailed in
        Item 7. Where existing MSA QHSE publications detail the topics listed, it is sufficient to either
        include them verbatim, or provide reference to where they may be found in MSA’s QHSE
        Manuals. The final project-specific QHSE plan shall satisfy MSA standards, PPL/PEDEC
        requirements and applicable HSE laws and regulations.
    MSA should establish an effective HSE system and implement it strictly.
    MSA must adhere to the standards and regulations of Health, Safe and Environment constituted
       by the local government.
    MSA must strictly adhere to the procedures / standards and regulations of Health, Safety and
       Environment promulgated by NIOC.
    MSA should comply with all applicable local government regulations including guidelines and
       recommended practices and any other Iranian authorities having jurisdiction.
    All personnel should respect the local culture, traditions and social values of the indigenous
        population of the Iran and South Azadegan region.
    MSA shall hire qualified and experienced worker and familiar his personnel with hazards
         especially with H2S and Mine and UXO (unexploded ordinance) according to related procedure.
    MSA shall conduct proper training about UXO (unexploded ordinance) and mine awareness to
           all personnel

 12.2 Health & Safety Management

As per national/local and client (PPL/PEDEC) standards and MSA/OEOC HSE plan. Following are
minimum requirement for the AZNS project:
    MSA/OEOC shall take all necessary safety measures in relation to the WORK to be provided
       and shall conduct itself and its work force in such a way as to comply at all times with the
         provisions of the national and local health and safety regulations pertinent to the contracted
       Scope of WORK.
    MSA/OEOC shall also comply with all and any of PPL/PEDEC’S safety standards and
        requirements as particularly applicable to the Operating Area and/or as may be imposed by
      PPL/PEDEC from time to time.
    MSA/OEOC and its subcontractors shall take all necessary steps to provide a safe and healthy
       working environment for employees, PPL/PEDEC’s personnel and third parties in the
        performance of the WORK.
    MSA/OEOC shall operate a management system that ensures:
    MSA/OEOC’s and sub contractors’ personnel operate safe systems of work at all times for the
        performance of the WORK.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
           South Azadegan Oilfield Project

   Contract No:          F – 20 Well Drilling Program                  Page 152 of 181

    Hazards associated with the work are identified and appropriate actions are taken to prevent
         accidents.
    Risks to safety and health of personnel involved in the WORK are identified and assessed, and
       any actions and measurements are implemented to either eliminate or reduce these risks to a
         level that is considered as low as reasonably practicable, given the nature of the WORK.
    Personnel are aware of the identified hazards and risks to their safety and health and to the
         safety and health of others. The detailed safe working procedures established by MSA/OEOC to
        ensure that the WORK will be conducted with due regard to the QHSE aspects, shall be detailed
         or referenced in the QHSE plan. Copies of the source documents shall be provided to
      PPL/PEDEC if the procedures are referenced only.

QHSE Representative:
    MSA/OEOC shall provide a dedicated, on-crew QHSE Representative. The primary
         responsibility of the QHSE Representative shall include, but not be limited to, the maintenance
       and monitoring of the implementation of HSE guidelines and procedures and where required
        provide HSE awareness training for the crew. Such activities shall be approved and actively
        supported by MSA/OEOC at corporate management level.

Training and Competence:
    MSA/OEOC shall ensure that its employees and those of its subcontractors are trained and
        competent to perform the WORK in a safe and environmentally responsible manner. Such
         training and demonstration of competence will include but not be limited to vocational, safety,
        emergency, environmental and other training required by regulatory agencies and IADC/OGP
         guidelines. MSA/OEOC’s senior staff shall have received training in safety management.

Accident/Incident Reporting:
    MSA/OEOC shall report all incidents. Any hazardous incident/near miss involving
       PPL/PEDEC, MSA/OEOC’s, or any third-party personnel, plant or equipment, shall be reported
         to PPL/PEDEC, whether or not injury to personnel or damage/loss to plant or equipment
         resulted. The following incidents shall be verbally reported immediately to the on-site
      PPL/PEDEC Representative(s): damage to property or equipment belonging to PPL/PEDEC,
         third parties, MSA/OEOC or its subcontractors; injury to personnel of PPL/PEDEC, third
          parties, subcontractors or MSA/OEOC; near misses which may have had serious potential
        consequences; environmental damage; media attention. An initial written report, containing
         factual information only, shall be prepared by MSA/OEOC and delivered to the PPL/PEDEC
        Representative within twenty-four (24) hours of the incident.
    PPL/PEDEC reserves the right to participate in the investigation of any incident arising out of
        performance of the WORK.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
           South Azadegan Oilfield Project

   Contract No:          F – 20 Well Drilling Program                  Page 153 of 181

    MSA/OEOC and all subcontractors shall have an accident and incident reporting system, details
         of which shall be included in the QHSE plan.
    MSA/OEOC shall maintain and forward to PPL/PEDEC, a monthly summary of its safety
        performance.
    MSA/OEOC shall prepare and forward to PPL/PEDEC, a weekly report stating all incidents and
        near misses that occurred during that week.
    All MSA/OEOC and subcontractor personnel and PPL/PEDEC Representative shall attend
         safety meetings on a regular basis. PPL/PEDEC Representative shall be given a copy of the
        minutes of the meetings.

Safety Equipment & Personal Protective Equipment (PPE) requirements:
    MSA/OEOC shall ensure that appropriate PPE compliant to national standards is supplied to
      MSA/OEOC’s personnel involved in the WORK. PPL/PEDEC intends to use water-based mud
       and hence MSA/OEOC’s Personnel that may be exposed to this must be provided with
         appropriate PPE.
    MSA/OEOC shall be solely liable for all costs in equipping its own and all other personnel on
         contract to, or under the control of, MSA/OEOC with the required safety equipment.
     MSA/OEOC will provide to all MSA/OEOC’s Personnel appropriate PPE throughout the
     WORK. Any defective PPE will be replaced without delay. Personnel without the correct PPE
         will not be allowed to continue work, until replacement PPE has been issued. If any person is
        disallowed to work owing to inadequate PPE, then PPL/PEDEC will deduct his day- rate from
      MSA/OEOC’s invoice.
    MSA/OEOC shall detail in the QHSE plan the PPE proposed for issue to the crew. This
        proposal should include the arrangements for replacement (due to wear and tear and damage) of
          this equipment.
    This well is committed to develop for oil in a responsible manner that promotes employees' and
         local inhabitants' health.
    A trained Paramedic and proper MEDIVAC equipment and ambulance shall be stationed on the
          drilling rig during the drilling campaign.
    An emergency medical evacuation plan shall be coordinated, and shall keep in touch with
       emergency response centers manned by qualified doctor 24-hours per day in Tehran or Ahwaz
          city.
    Medical emergency  report and  investigation  shall be performed  as soon as  possible  in
        accordance with Medical Emergency Procedures.
    MSA/OEOC shall, as a minimum, conduct the WORK and provide personnel, equipment,
          facilities and services  consistent with good  oil  field  practices and OGP Guidelines. An
        assessment of the Contract Area with regard to “Environmental and Occupational Health Risks”
       and “Local Medical Support” shall be provided by MSA/OEOC in the QHSE plan. This

        assessment and  the  resulting  “Health  Risk Assessment  Score”  will be  discussed  with
       PPL/PEDEC, and a final assessment will be agreed.
    To ensure the fitness of those personnel engaged to undertake the WORK, MSA/OEOC shall
        conduct periodic medical examinations of its staff and ensure that its subcontractors’ personnel
         are also subject to periodic medical examination. As a minimum this shall include pre-hire
        medical examinations of temporary local personnel and bi-annual medical examinations of its
        permanent and expatriate staff. Evidence should be made available to PPL/PEDEC to verify an
         appropriate program of immunizations consistent with the recommendations of the World
        Health Organization for the area of operations.
    MSA/OEOC shall be responsible for the cost of medical examinations as required to provide
      PPL/PEDEC with medical certificates which confirm the fitness of MSA/OEOC’s assigned
        personnel for the WORK. Each drilling rig shall be equipped with a hospital, doctor and an
       ambulance type-2 level.

Drug Abuse and Control:
    MSA/OEOC warrants that it shall obtain valid written consent of all Personnel to a search of
        property of such Personnel when PPL/PEDEC suspects that such Personnel have in their
        possession drugs and/or other prohibited substances or any other item likely to prejudice safety
         or security. PPL/PEDE will provide a representative to attend the search.
    MSA/OEOC warrants that  it shall obtain valid written consent of all Personnel to medical
         testing for the presence of drugs or other prohibited substances.
    MSA/OEOC further agrees that any Personnel who refuse to provide such consent or consents
         or withdraws such consent or consents shall at PPL/PEDEC’s option be removed from the
     WORK and shall not, at PPL/PEDEC’s option, be assigned to any future services to be provided
       by MSA/OEOC for PPL/PEDEC.
    MSA/OEOC further agrees that  it shall immediately notify PPL/PEDEC in the event that
     MSA/OEOC becomes aware that any of its Personnel has been found in possession of drugs
        and/or any other prohibited substance. MSA/OEOC is required to notify PPL/PEDEC in the
        event that any Personnel provided for the WORK have previously been found in possession, or
        have abused drugs and/or other substances prohibited at Location. The acceptance of such
        Personnel for the WORK shall be at the sole discretion of PPL/PEDEC.

Camp Standards:
    MSA/OEOC shall abide by all statutory Iranian governmental health and safety regulations, and
        maintain acceptable standards for cleanliness and hygiene for quarters, camps and the Operating
        Area(s) in general. MSA/OEOC shall detail the standards and facilities proposed for its camp in
         the QHSE plan.

Emergency Preparedness:
    MSA/OEOC and its subcontractors shall have emergency preparedness and response plans plus
        procedures available at all times throughout the duration of the CONTRACT.
    MSA/OEOC shall take all necessary measures to ensure that:
    I. Potential emergency situations have been identified and assessed;
    II. An emergency response plan, including clear responsibilities, is established;
    III. MSA/OEOC’s management, employees and third parties are aware of response procedures
     and responsibilities;
   IV. Regular drills and exercises are carried out to test the emergency response organization and
     procedures and that any resultant issues are properly addressed.
    MSA/OEOC shall notify PPL/PEDEC immediately with respect to any emergency situation
     and shall provide regular updates on emergency planning procedures, tests etc.
    MSA/OEOC  shall  detail in the QHSE plan a  list of potential contingencies for which
     emergency response procedures are planned. The QHSE plan should also detail a schedule of
     proposed exercises and drills to verify the effectiveness of the proposed arrangements in place.
     Development and implementation of the above plan  shall incorporate any PPL/PEDEC
      assigned personnel to the project.

 12.3 Environmental Management

    COMPANY shall comply with any and all laws, rules and regulations of governmental agencies
        having  jurisdiction, which now  exist  or may be promulgated  during  the  term  of  the
      CONTRACT, relating to the control and prevention of damage to the environment.
    Without prejudice to above, COMPANY  shall ensure  that  all personnel use the highest
         prevailing standards of care and diligence to prevent unlawful disposal of all waste oil, water
       and other waste materials or fluids which may accumulate or be caused by COMPANY’s
        performance of the CONTRACT. COMPANY shall not dispose of any objects, scrap, waste,
         rubbish, equipment, fluids or fuel into the surrounding area or allow anything to be stored loose
       where it can be driven into surrounding areas by wind. COMPANY shall take all reasonable
        precautions to prevent pollution of any nature or kind from its performance of the WORK.
     COMPANY shall have contingency plans and procedures in place for such events.
    In the event of any disposal prohibited by Item 3.2 above, whether by dumping, spilling,
        emission or otherwise arising from COMPANY’s performance or non-performance of the
      CONTRACT, COMPANY shall be fully liable for and bear the costs of removal of any such
         item(s) dumped, spilled, emitted or otherwise disposed regardless of the sole, concurrent or
         contributory negligence or breach of duty (statutory or otherwise) of PPL/PEDEC. In the event
         of any such disposal, COMPANY shall immediately undertake all reasonable remedial and
         protective measures required in such circumstances.
    COMPANY shall notify PPL/PEDEC immediately with respect to any pollution, loss, damage,
        claim or demand  (or occurrence which may give  rise  to  the same)  resulting from the
      CONTRACT. COMPANY shall report to PPL/PEDEC any incidents of non-compliance with
